1148	2025-08-16 10:02:49	Farrell	0	1. Modify or Replace the Transmission Crossmember
- Trim or notch the top of the crossmember where the yoke contacts it
- Reinforce the area with boxed steel or gussets to maintain strength
- Alternatively, fabricate a custom crossmember with a drop or relief for the yoke

2. Adjust Transfer Case Position
- Use clocking rings or adapter plates to rotate or slightly reposition the Dana 20
- This can shift the yoke away from the interference point, but may affect driveshaft angles

3. Body or Suspension Lift
- If you’re open to it, a 1–2" body lift can create more clearance between the drivetrain and frame
- A suspension lift might help too, but could complicate driveshaft geometry

4. Swap to a Shorter Output Yoke
- Some aftermarket yokes (e.g., low-profile CV yokes) offer more clearance
- Check if your current yoke is oversized or if a shorter one is available for the Dana 20

5. Engine and Transmission Mount Adjustments
- Verify that your engine mounts and transmission adapter are properly aligned
- Slightly raising or lowering the transmission tail with shims or spacers can help reposition the transfer case

908	2025-09-04 16:30:55	Farrell	0	🔍 Common Causes of No Reverse in a C6 Transmission

1. Direct Clutch Pack Failure
- Reverse relies on the direct clutch (also used for 3rd gear).
- If reverse and 3rd are both missing, this is your prime suspect.
- Symptoms: Reverse feels like neutral, or slight lurching with throttle but no engagement.

2. Low-Reverse Band or Servo Malfunction
- The low-reverse band must engage properly.
- A stuck or misadjusted servo can prevent band application.
- Solution: Check servo piston and linkage; air-check band engagement if possible.

3. Valve Body Issues
- A misaligned or dirty valve body can block fluid flow to reverse circuits.
- Solution: Drop the pan, inspect valve body gaskets, passages, and torque specs.

4. Improper Assembly or Worn Seals
- If the rebuild wasn’t meticulous, seals on the direct drum may be leaking.
- Solution: Air-check the direct drum to confirm clutch engagement. If it leaks, it’s teardown time.

5. Incorrect Fluid Level
- Too much fluid can cause aeration; too little causes pressure loss.
- Check: With engine running, trans in park, fluid should be at the full mark. Use Type F fluid only.	2	1937

4674	2025-09-04 16:35:59	Farrell	0	Diagnostic Checklist

| Test/Check | What to Look For | Tools Needed |
|------------|------------------|--------------|
| Fluid Level | Type F, correct level | Dipstick |
| Reverse Engagement | Lurching, stalling, or neutral feel | Visual + throttle test |
| 3rd Gear Function | If missing, direct clutch is suspect | Road test |
| Servo Operation | Band engagement | Air-check or manual inspection |
| Valve Body | Clean, aligned, torqued | Torque wrench, gasket set |
| Transmission Pressure | Low pressure = internal leak | Pressure gauge |

---

🛠️ Suggested Next Steps

1. Road Test: Confirm if 3rd gear is also missing.
2. Drop the Pan: Inspect fluid, valve body, and servo.
3. Air Check Drums: Especially direct clutch and low-reverse band.
4. Review Rebuild Specs: Was the direct drum rebuilt with new seals and clutches?
5. Consider Pulling the Trans: If internal failure is confirmed, it may need rework.

These were common on late-'60s to early-'70s Ford engines and were designed to reroute vacuum based on coolant temperature—essentially advancing timing when the engine gets hot to increase idle speed and cooling efficiency.	2	4422

3093	2025-09-04 16:55:31	Farrell	0	- Yes, run a vacuum line from the carburetor’s ported vacuum to the valve, then from the valve to the distributor vacuum advance.
- This setup mimics original emissions and cooling behavior.

🔧 If You Want Simplicity and Performance:
- Skip the valve and run direct vacuum from the carb’s timed/ported vacuum port (usually on the passenger side of the carb base) straight to the distributor.
- This gives smoother idle and better throttle response, especially with a performance cam or aftermarket ignition.	2	4630

9755	2025-10-22 10:25:00	Farrell	0	1. Section 179 Deduction
- Eligibility: Vehicle must be used more than 50% for business and placed in service during the tax year.
- Deduction Limit: Up to $1,220,000 in 2025 for qualifying property.
- Classic Vehicles: If the Bronco is restored or modified, it may still qualify if used for business and not considered a collectible or investment.

2. Bonus Depreciation (100%)
- Applies to: New or used vehicles acquired and used in business.
- Deduction: 100% of the purchase price in the first year (2025), regardless of cost.
- Timeframe: Available through 2026 under the extended Trump tax bill.

3. Advertising Expense Deduction
- If the Bronco is used solely for promotional display, wrapped with branding, and not driven for personal use, it may be treated as a marketing expense.
- This could allow full deduction as an ordinary business expense under IRS rules.	2	9718

11287	2025-11-17 12:09:38	Farrell	0	Likely Causes of White Smoke After Rollover
- Oil on the heads/exhaust system  
  - During a rollover, oil can slosh into the intake, combustion chambers, or onto hot exhaust surfaces. This often burns off as white/blue smoke at idle.
- Coolant intrusion (head gasket or intake leak)  
  - White smoke can also mean coolant is entering the combustion chamber. But since everything sounds normal and this happened after a rollover, oil contamination is more likely.
- Old fuel (18 months)  
  - Gasoline degrades, loses volatility, and can cause poor combustion, fouling plugs, or smoky exhaust. It’s definitely past usable life.
- Residual fluids in intake/exhaust  
  - Oil or fuel trapped in intake runners or mufflers can burn off slowly, especially at idle.

---

✅ Immediate Steps You Should Take
- Change the oil & filter  
  - After a rollover, oil may have aerated or picked up contaminants. Fresh oil ensures you’re not circulating debris.
- Drain and replace fuel  
  - Old fuel should be completely removed. Refill with fresh super unleaded (high octane) to match the Coyote motor’s needs.
- Inspect air intake & throttle body  
  - Look for oil residue in the intake tract. Clean if necessary.
- Check spark plugs  
  - If oil fouled them, replace. Cheap insurance against misfires.
- Run engine to operating temp  
  - After fluids are replaced, let it idle and then drive gently. If smoke clears, it was residual oil. If it persists, investigate coolant leaks.	2	11218
